    Ratio 1.33.
    Wait, $k=6$ is $0 \pmod 3$. Case 3 with $m=2$.
    $q^3 = \frac{2+2}{2+1} = 4/3$.
    Yes.
    So max ratio is for smallest indices of fully contained blocks.
    Smallest possible fully contained blocks are $B_2, B_3$.
    Because $B_1$ cannot be fully contained with just 1 other block without dragging in a 3rd block?
    Let's verify.
    To contain $B_1$ (1..3), range must start $\le 1$.
    Since range length 9, range is 1..9.
    Contains $B_1, B_2, B_3$.
    3 blocks $\implies$ impossible.
    So $B_1$ can never be fully contained.
    So fully contained blocks must be from $B_2$ onwards.
    To maximize ratio $T_{n+1}/T_n = (n+1)/n$, we need smallest $n$.
    Smallest available $n$ for a pair of blocks is $n=2$ (blocks $B_2, B_3$).
    Ratio $3/2 = 1.5$.
    Next pair $B_3, B_4$ ratio $4/3 \approx 1.33$.
    So max $q^3 = 1.5$.
    Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    **Is it possible to have 0 full blocks?**
    If range doesn't contain any full block.
    Range length 9.
    Blocks size 3.
    Impossible to avoid covering a full block?
    Indices $k, \dots, k+8$.
    Consider modulo 3 classes.
    Every block has one of each residue?
    $B_n = \{3n-2, 3n-1, 3n\} \equiv \{1, 2, 0\} \pmod 3$.
    Any sequence of 3 consecutive integers is a block.
    Any sequence of 9 consecutive integers contains 3 sequences of 3 consecutive integers.
    $a_k, a_{k+1}, a_{k+2}$ (Block 1)
    $a_{k+3}, a_{k+4}, a_{k+5}$ (Block 2)
    $a_{k+6}, a_{k+7}, a_{k+8}$ (Block 3)
    These are triplets of consecutive terms.
    Do they align with $B_n$?
    Only if $k \equiv 1 \pmod 3$.
    If $k \equiv 2$, triplets are shifted.
    Triplet 1: $k, k+1, k+2 \equiv 2, 0, 1$. Not a block.
    Triplet 2: $k+3, \dots \equiv 2, 0, 1$.
    Triplet 3: $k+6, \dots \equiv 2, 0, 1$.
    However, the union of these triplets covers the range.
    But we need sums of *blocks* $B_n$ to be $2n$.
    The sums of these shifted triplets are not constrained directly.
    But the terms are the same.
    So if $k \equiv 2$, we have terms $x, xq, \dots$.
    Sums of triplets $(x, xq, xq^2)$ etc are not constrained.
    But we found that partial blocks constraints are loose.
    But full blocks constraints are rigid.
    In Case 2 ($k \equiv 2$), we found 2 full blocks $B_{m+2}, B_{m+3}$.
    Wait, if $k=2$ ($m=0$), range 2..10.
    Full blocks $B_2$ (4..6) and $B_3$ (7..9).
    Indices 4,5,6 are $xq^2, xq^3, xq^4$.
    Indices 7,8,9 are $xq^5, xq^6, xq^7$.
    Sums constrained.
    What if we shift $k$ so that NO block is fully contained?
    Is that possible?
    Range length 9.
    Blocks are $[1,3], [4,6], [7,9], [10,12] \dots$.
    Range $[k, k+8]$.
    If $k=2$, range $[2, 10]$.
    $[1,3] \cap [2,10] = [2,3]$ (size 2).
    $[4,6] \subset [2,10]$ (size 3). Fully contained.
    So we can't avoid full blocks.
    With length 9, you must cover at least 2 full blocks.
    Proof:
    Let range be $I$. Length 9.
    Let $B$ be a block of length 3.
    If $I$ contains no full block, then for every $B_n$, $B_n \not\subset I$.
    This means $I$ "cuts" every block.
    But blocks are contiguous.
    If $I$ cuts $B_n$, it must contain some part.
    If it doesn't contain $B_n$ fully, it must miss at least one element of $B_n$.
    Actually, simpler:
    Number of integers in $I$ is 9.
    Number of integers in 3 blocks is 9.
    If $I$ doesn't contain 3 blocks, it must be shifted.
    If shifted by 1 ($k=2$), it contains 2 full blocks.
    If shifted by 2 ($k=3$), it contains 2 full blocks.
    So always at least 2 full blocks.
    Wait, check $k=3$. Range 3..11.
    $B_1$ (1..3) overlap 3.
    $B_2$ (4..6) inside.
    $B_3$ (7..9) inside.
    $B_4$ (10..12) overlap 10,11.
    2 full blocks.
    Check $k=4$. Range 4..12.
    $B_2$ inside.
    $B_3$ inside.
    $B_4$ inside.
    3 full blocks.
    So yes, always at least 2 full blocks.
    And if 3 full blocks, impossible.
    So must have exactly 2 full blocks.
    The blocks must be consecutive.
    Let them be $B_n, B_{n+1}$.
    Ratio constraint $q^3 = T_{n+1}/T_n = (n+1)/n$.
    We need to maximize this ratio.
    Max ratio is for smallest $n$.
    Can we have $n=1$?
    Blocks $B_1, B_2$.
    Requires $B_1, B_2$ fully contained.
    Range must cover $[1, 6]$.
    Min range $[1, 6]$ length 6.
    But range length 9.
    So range must extend to 9 or beyond.
    If range is $[1, 9]$, contains $B_3$. 3 blocks. Impossible.
    If range is $[-2, 6]$? Indices must be positive.
    So $k \ge 1$.
    So range starts $\ge 1$.
    If range starts at 1, it is $[1, 9]$. 3 blocks.
    If range starts at 2, it is $[2, 10]$.
    Contains $B_2, B_3$. ($n=2$).
    So $n=1$ is impossible because containing $B_1$ fully forces $k=1$, which forces 3 blocks.
    Wait, does containing $B_1$ fully force $k=1$?
    $B_1 = \{1, 2, 3\}$.
    If $\{1, 2, 3\} \subset \{k, \dots, k+8\}$, then $k \le 1$ and $k+8 \ge 3$.
    $k \le 1$.
    Since $k \in \mathbb{N}^*$, $k=1$.
    Yes.
    So if $B_1$ is fully contained, $k=1$.
    Then range is $[1, 9]$.
    Contains $B_3$ (7,8,9).
    So 3 blocks.
    Contradiction.
    So $B_1$ cannot be fully contained.
    So $n$ must be $\ge 2$.
    Max ratio at $n=2$.
    Ratio $3/2$.
    So max $q^3 = 1.5$.

    **Is there any loophole?**
    What if $q < 0$?
    We ruled it out.
    What if $x=0$?
    Ruled out.
    What if $1+q+q^2=0$?
    Ruled out.
    What if terms are not distinct?
    Doesn't matter.
    What if $q=1$?
    Ratio 1. $1.5 \ne 1$.
    What if $T_n$ is not $2n$?
    Calculated $T_n = 2n$ from problem statement. Correct.

    **Let's check the calculation of $T_n$ again.**
    $S_{3n} = n^2+n$.
    $S_3 = 2$.
    $S_6 = 6$.
    $S_9 = 12$.
    $T_1 = 2$.
    $T_2 = 4$.
    $T_3 = 6$.
    $T_n = 2n$.
    Correct.

    **Let's check the block indices.**
    $B_n$ terms $a_{3n-2}, a_{3n-1}, a_{3n}$.
    Sum $T_n$.
    Correct.

    **Let's check the GP indexing.**
    GP terms $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$.
    Terms are $x, xq, \dots, xq^8$.
    Correct.

    **Let's check the ratio logic.**
    If $B_n, B_{n+1}$ fully contained.
    $B_n$ terms are $a_{3n-2}, a_{3n-1}, a_{3n}$.
    These are consecutive terms in the sequence.
    Are they consecutive in the GP?
    GP terms are consecutive in sequence.
    So yes, any 3 consecutive terms in sequence that are inside GP are consecutive in GP.
    Let start of $B_n$ be at index $j$ in GP.
    $a_{3n-2} = x q^j$.
    $a_{3n-1} = x q^{j+1}$.
    $a_{3n} = x q^{j+2}$.
    Sum $S_n = x q^j (1+q+q^2)$.
    $B_{n+1}$ starts at index $3n+1$.
    Difference in index is 3.
    So terms are shifted by 3 in GP.
    $a_{3n+1} = x q^{j+3}$.
    Sum $S_{n+1} = x q^{j+3} (1+q+q^2) = q^3 S_n$.
    Correct.
    So $T_{n+1} = q^3 T_n$.
    $2(n+1) = q^3 (2n)$.
    $q^3 = \frac{n+1}{n}$.
    Correct.

    **Let's check the constraint on $n$ again.**
    We established $n \ge 2$.
    Why?
    Because $n=1$ implies $B_1$ fully contained.
    $B_1$ indices 1,2,3.
    GP range $[k, k+8]$.
    $1,2,3 \in [k, k+8] \implies k \le 1$.
    $k \ge 1 \implies k=1$.
    GP range $[1, 9]$.
    Contains indices 7,8,9.
    These form $B_3$.
    So $B_3$ is fully contained.
    So we have $B_1, B_2, B_3$ fully contained.
    Implies $q^3 = T_2/T_1 = 2$ AND $q^3 = T_3/T_2 = 1.5$.
    Contradiction.
    So $B_1$ cannot be fully contained.
    Thus $n \ge 2$.
    Max ratio at $n=2$ is $1.5$.
    $q^3 = 1.5$.
    $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    **Is it possible that $B_n$ and $B_{n+2}$ are fully contained but not $B_{n+1}$?**
    $B_n$ indices $[3n-2, 3n]$.
    $B_{n+1}$ indices $[3n+1, 3n+3]$.
    $B_{n+2}$ indices $[3n+4, 3n+6]$.
    If GP range contains $B_n$ and $B_{n+2}$, it must contain indices $3n-2$ through $3n+6$.
    This interval includes $[3n+1, 3n+3]$ which is $B_{n+1}$.
    Since GP range is an interval (consecutive integers), it must contain everything between min and max indices.
    So it must contain $B_{n+1}$.
    So blocks must be consecutive.

    **Is it possible that the sum ratio logic fails?**
    Only if $1+q+q^2 = 0$ or $x=0$.
    Checked.

    **Is there any other interpretation?**
    Maybe $q$ is not constant? "common ratio $q$". Constant.
    Maybe $a_n$ not real? "real number $q$". $a_n$ usually real in such context, but even if complex, sum $S_{3n}$ is real, so likely $a_n$ real or conjugate pairs. But "sequence" usually implies real unless specified.
    Actually if $a_n$ complex, $S_{3n}$ real implies sum is real.
    But "real number $q$" suggests we are in real domain.
    Also standard contest math assumes real sequences unless specified.

    **Final check of the question phrasing.**
    "14. 设实数q满足：存在数列{a_n}，使得对于任意n \in N^*，均有a_1 + a_2 + \cdots + a_{3n} = n^2 + n，且{a_n}中有某连续9项a_k, a_{k+1}, \cdots, a_{k+8}是公比为q的等比数列。则q的最大值为__________."
    Everything seems covered.

    Result $q = \sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    Can be written as $\frac{\sqrt[3]{12}}{2}$ or $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    Usually $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ is acceptable. Or $\frac{3}{2\sqrt[3]{4}}$? No.
    Rationalizing denominator: $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}} \cdot \frac{\sqrt[3]{4}}{\sqrt[3]{4}} = \frac{\sqrt[3]{12}}{2}$.
    Let's stick to $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ or $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    Wait, is there a nicer form?
    Maybe $q$ is rational? No.
    Maybe I missed a case where $q$ is larger?
    What if $q$ is negative?
    We said $q>0$.
    Let's rigorously check $q<0$.
    $T_n = 2n > 0$.
    $S_n = x q^j (1+q+q^2)$.
    $1+q+q^2 = (q+1/2)^2 + 3/4 > 0$.
    So sign of $S_n$ is sign of $x q^j$.
    $S_{n+1} = x q^{j+3} (1+q+q^2) = q^3 S_n$.
    Since $S_n, S_{n+1} > 0$, $q^3$ must be positive.
    So $q > 0$.
    So no negative solutions.

    What if $x < 0$?
    Then $q^j$ must be negative (if $j$ odd) to make $S_n > 0$.
    $q^{j+3}$ would have same sign as $q^j$ (since $q^3 > 0$).
    So signs consistent.
    But we found $x$ must be positive for $S_n > 0$ if $j$ is even?
    Wait.
    $S_n = x q^j (1+q+q^2)$.
    $1+q+q^2 > 0$.
    If $x < 0$, we need $q^j < 0$.
    Since $q > 0$, $q^j > 0$.
    So $x$ cannot be negative.
    So $x > 0$.
    So everything is positive.

    **Wait, check Case 2 again.**
    $B_2$ terms $a_4, a_5, a_6$.
    $k=2$. GP starts $a_2$.
    $a_2=x$.
    $a_4 = x q^2$.
    $j=2$ (exponent).
    $S_2 = x q^2 (1+q+q^2)$.
    Since $x>0, q>0$, $S_2 > 0$.
    Consistent.

    **Double check $m=0$ in Case 2.**
    $k=2$.
    $B_2$ terms $x q^2, x q^3, x q^4$.
    $S_2 = x q^2(1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    $B_3$ terms $x q^5, x q^6, x q^7$.
    $S_3 = x q^5(1+q+q^2) = 6$.
    $S_3/S_2 = q^3 = 1.5$.
    $x q^2(1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    We need to solve for $x$.
    $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5} \approx 1.145$.
    $q^2 \approx 1.31$.
    $1+q+q^2 \approx 3.455$.
    $x \approx 4 / (1.31 \times 3.455) \approx 4 / 4.526 \approx 0.88$.
    $x > 0$.
    So valid.

    **Check partial block $B_1$ sum.**
    $B_1$ terms $a_1, a_2, a_3$.
    $a_2 = x$, $a_3 = xq$.
    Sum $a_1 + x + xq = 2$.
    $a_1 = 2 - x(1+q)$.
    $x(1+q) \approx 0.88(2.145) \approx 1.89$.
    $a_1 \approx 0.11$.
    Valid.

    **Check partial block $B_4$ sum.**
    $B_4$ terms $a_{10}, a_{11}, a_{12}$.
    $a_{10} = x q^8$.
    $x q^8 = x q^2 \cdot q^6 = x q^2 (1.5)^2 = x q^2 \cdot 2.25$.
    $x q^2 \approx 1.158$ (term $a_4$).
    Wait, $x q^2$ is term $a_4$.
    $a_4 \approx 1.158$.
    $a_{10} = a_4 q^6 = 1.158 \times 2.25 \approx 2.60$.
    Sum $B_4 = 2.60 + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$.
    $a_{11} + a_{12} = 5.4$.
    Valid.

    So the solution holds.
    Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    **Is there any other constraint?**
    "continuous 9 terms".
    Yes, we used that.
    "real number q".
    Yes.
    "sequence".
    Yes.

    **Let's check if $q$ can be larger if we choose a different alignment?**
    We checked all alignments modulo 3.
    Alignment 1 ($k \equiv 1$): 3 blocks $\implies$ impossible.
    Alignment 2 ($k \equiv 2$): 2 blocks ($B_{m+2}, B_{m+3}$). Max ratio 1.5.
    Alignment 3 ($k \equiv 0$): 2 blocks ($B_{m+1}, B_{m+2}$). Max ratio 1.5.
    Wait, in Alignment 3, $k=3m$.
    If $m=0$, $k=0$ invalid.
    If $m=1$, $k=3$.
    Blocks $B_2, B_3$.
    Ratio 1.5.
    Wait, if $k=3$, range 3..11.
    $B_1$ (1..3) overlap 3.
    $B_2$ (4..6) inside.
    $B_3$ (7..9) inside.
    $B_4$ (10..12) overlap 10,11.
    So blocks $B_2, B_3$ fully contained.
    Ratio $T_3/T_2 = 1.5$.
    Same max.

    **Could we have $k$ such that blocks are $B_1, B_2$ but not $B_3$?**
    Requires range covering 1..6 but not 7..9.
    Range length 9.
    If it covers 1..6, it starts $\le 1$.
    Since $k \ge 1$, $k=1$.
    Range 1..9.
    Covers 7..9.
    So impossible.

    **Could we have blocks $B_0, B_1$?**
    $B_0$ doesn't exist.
    So minimal blocks are $B_1, B_2$ or $B_2, B_3$.
    $B_1, B_2$ impossible.
    $B_2, B_3$ possible.
    Max ratio 1.5.

    **Is it possible that $q$ is defined by a different relation?**
    Maybe $x(1+q+q^2)$ is not the sum?
    Sum of 3 consecutive terms in GP is always $a(1+q+q^2)$.
    Yes.
    Maybe blocks are not consecutive terms in GP?
    If a block is fully contained, its terms are consecutive in the sequence.
    Since GP is consecutive terms in sequence, block terms are consecutive in GP.
    Yes.
    So sum formula holds.

    **Is it possible that $q=1$ is allowed?**
    If $q=1$, $S_{n+1} = S_n$.
    $2(n+1) = 2n \implies 2=0$ impossible.
    So $q \ne 1$.

    **Is it possible that $q=-1$?**
    $S_{n+1} = -S_n$.
    $2(n+1) = -2n \implies 2n+2 = -2n \implies 4n = -2$ impossible.

    **Conclusion:**
    Max $q^3 = 1.5$.
    Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    The question asks for the maximum value.
    Answer format: likely $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ or $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    In Chinese math competitions, usually $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ or $\frac{\sqrt[3]{12}}{2}$.
    Let's write $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    Or maybe just decimal? No, exact form.
    Let's check if $q$ can be written as $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    Or $\frac{3}{2^{2/3}}$? No.
    $\sqrt[3]{3/2}$.

    Let's double check if $a_n$ must be integers?
    "实数q" (real number q).
    "数列{a_n}" (sequence).
    Usually implies real numbers.
    If integers, $x$ would have to be integer?
    $x \approx 0.88$, not integer.
    So sequence is real.

    One final check.
    Problem 14.
    Maybe there is a trick with $n^2+n$.
    $S_{3n} = n(n+1)$.
    $S_{3n} - S_{3n-3} = 2n$.
    Correct.
    Maybe I should write the answer as $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    Let's verify if there is any other interpretation of "consecutive 9 terms".
    Maybe indices $k, k+1, \dots, k+8$ don't have to be aligned with the block sums?
    We handled that.
    Maybe $q$ can be complex? "实数q". No.
    Maybe $a_n$ can be 0?
    If $a_n=0$, then $x=0$ or terms are 0.
    If $x=0$, sums are 0, impossible.
    So $a_n \ne 0$.

    Wait, what if the GP is $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$ but $q$ is such that terms are not monotonic?
    Doesn't matter.
    What if $q$ is large?
    If $q$ is large, terms grow fast.
    Sums grow fast.
    But sums are constrained to linear growth $2n$.
    This restricts $q$.
    Specifically, sum of 3 terms grows by factor $q^3$ every 3 indices.
    Sum of blocks grows by factor $\approx 1$ (ratio $(n+1)/n \to 1$).
    So $q^3$ must be close to 1.
    For small $n$, ratio is larger.
    Max ratio is 1.5.
    So $q^3$ max 1.5.
    Seems consistent.

    Let's quickly check $n=1$ block sum.
    $T_1 = 2$.
    If $q$ was large, say 2.
    Then next block sum would be $8$.
    But required is 4.
    Contradiction.
    So $q$ cannot be 2.
    So logic holds.

    Final Answer seems to be $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    Let's write it as $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$ or $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    Standard form $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ is good.
    Or $\frac{\sqrt[3]{12}}{2}$.
    I'll provide $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    Actually, looking at the options in such problems, usually simplified radical.
    $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$ is fine.
    Let's check if $q$ could be $\sqrt[3]{3/2}$.
    Yes.

    Wait, is there any possibility that the "consecutive 9 terms" are not $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$?
    "某连续9项" means "some consecutive 9 terms".
    Yes, $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$.

    Is it possible that $a_k$ is not the first term of the GP?
    "a_k, ..., a_{k+8} 是公比为q的等比数列".
    This means the sequence of these 9 terms is a GP.
    So $a_{k+1} = a_k q$, etc.
    Yes.

    Everything seems solid.

    Wait, I might have missed the case where the sum of 3 terms is 0?
    $T_n = 2n \ne 0$.
    So sums are never 0.

    One distinct possibility:
    Could the GP be "backwards"?
    i.e. $a_{k+8}, \dots, a_k$ is GP?
    "a_k, ..., a_{k+8} 是 ... 等比数列".
    Order matters. $a_{i+1}/a_i = q$.
    So standard forward GP.

    Final check on calculation of $m$ range.
    Case 2: $k=3m+2$.
    $k \ge 1 \implies 3m+2 \ge 1 \implies 3m \ge -1 \implies m \ge 0$.
    Wait, $m$ is integer?
    $k$ is integer.
    $3m = k-2$.
    So $m$ is integer.
    Correct.
    Max $q^3 = \frac{0+3}{0+2} = 1.5$.
    Case 3: $k=3m$.
    $k \ge 1 \implies 3m \ge 1 \implies m \ge 1/3$.
    $m$ integer $\implies m \ge 1$.
    Max $q^3 = \frac{1+2}{1+1} = 1.5$.
    Wait, formula for Case 3 was $q^3 = \frac{m+2}{m+1}$.
    For $m=1$, $3/2$.
    Correct.

    What if $k$ is very large?
    $q^3 \to 1$.
    So max is indeed at smallest valid $k$.
    Smallest valid $k$ in Case 2 is 2 ($m=0$).
    Smallest valid $k$ in Case 3 is 3 ($m=1$).
    Smallest valid $k$ in Case 1 is 1 ($m=0$), but impossible.
    So candidates are $k=2$ and $k=3$.
    $k=2 \implies q^3 = 1.5$.
    $k=3 \implies q^3 = 1.5$.
    Both give same max.

    Wait, check $k=2$ again.
    $k=2$. $m=0$.
    $q^3 = 3/2$.
    Terms $a_2, \dots, a_{10}$.
    $B_2$ sum 4. $B_3$ sum 6.
    $B_2$ terms $a_4, a_5, a_6$.
    $a_4 = a_2 q^2$.
    $a_5 = a_2 q^3$.
    $a_6 = a_2 q^4$.
    Sum $a_2 q^2(1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    $B_3$ terms $a_7, a_8, a_9$.
    $a_7 = a_2 q^5$.
    Sum $a_2 q^5(1+q+q^2) = 6$.
    Ratio $q^3 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    Consistent.
    Check $B_1$ sum 2.
    $a_1 + a_2 + a_3 = 2$.
    $a_2 = x, a_3 = xq$.
    $a_1 = 2 - x(1+q)$.
    Check $B_4$ sum 8.
    $a_{10} + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$.
    $a_{10} = x q^8$.
    $a_{11}, a_{12}$ free.
    Consistent.

    Check $k=3$.
    $k=3$. $m=1$.
    $q^3 = 3/2$.
    Terms $a_3, \dots, a_{11}$.
    $B_2$ sum 4. $B_3$ sum 6.
    $B_2$ terms $a_4, a_5, a_6$.
    $a_4 = a_3 q$.
    $a_5 = a_3 q^2$.
    $a_6 = a_3 q^3$.
    Sum $a_3 q(1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    $B_3$ terms $a_7, a_8, a_9$.
    $a_7 = a_3 q^4$.
    Sum $a_3 q^4(1+q+q^2) = 6$.
    Ratio $q^3 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    Consistent.
    Check $B_1$ sum 2.
    $a_1 + a_2 + a_3 = 2$.
    $a_3 = x$.
    $a_1, a_2$ free.
    $a_1 + a_2 = 2 - x$.
    Consistent.
    Check $B_4$ sum 8.
    $a_{10} + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$.
    $a_{10} = x q^7$.
    $a_{11}, a_{12}$ free.
    Consistent.

    Both $k=2$ and $k=3$ work with $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    Max value is $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    Is there any constraint that $a_n$ must be positive?
    No.
    But we found $x>0$ is required.
    If $x<0$, $a_2 < 0$, $a_3 < 0$.
    $a_1$ could be large positive.
    But for $B_2$, sum is 4.
    $a_4, a_5, a_6$ would be negative (since $x<0, q>0$).
    Sum would be negative.
    But sum must be 4.
    Contradiction.
    So $x$ must be positive.
    So all GP terms positive.
    So sequence has positive terms in that range.

    Final Answer seems robust.
    $q_{max} = \sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
